Packaging finding: the body-bolster underside is 482.7 mm above rail in the retained study. The 500 and 600 mm wheel cases place axle centers at 250 and 300 mm, leaving datum differences of 232.7 and 182.7 mm. Those differences are not available spring height: seats, bearing housing, frame structure, travel and the body bearing stack still occupy space. The bolster lies between axles, so interference must be checked where the actual moving volumes overlap.
The 32-inch floor reference has no accepted empty/loaded state. That must be resolved before station interfaces and suspension seat elevations are fixed.
Spring loads and height change
Illustrative calculation: eight coils per truck, two per axlebox; 900 kg of unsprung mass per truck; uniform loading; a 2.0 Hz loaded vertical-bounce study point. These are explicit trial inputs, not selected springs, measured mass or a ride-comfort approval. Each row is sized independently to that study point.

For the regular coach example, the spring compression changes from 28.5 mm at tare to 62.1 mm at the upper mass case. That 33.6 mm body-height change has to be coordinated with boarding, stairs and accessible boarding equipment. It does not include dynamic bounce, roll, wheel wear or tolerances.
Equations, sensitivity and limits
For each truck: sprung mass = half coach mass − unsprung mass. With eight equal coils, force per coil = sprung mass × 9.80665 / 8. Rate k = (2πf)² × sprung mass / 8000 in N/mm. Static compression = force / k. The saved calculation evaluates 24 combinations: two coaches, four unsprung-mass trials (300, 600, 900, 1,200 kg) and three loaded frequency trials (1.8, 2.0, 2.2 Hz).
The model treats vertical heave only. Uneven boarding, track twist, roll/pitch, guide friction, damping, brake reaction and fatigue require separate checks. Independent springs do not by themselves prove adequate equalization. A 0% route grade does not eliminate track twist.
Body-center-bearing loads require the complete truck mass to be subtracted from the body-supported share. The primary-spring mass calculation cannot be reused directly as the center-bearing load.

Compare a guided primary-sprung arrangement with equalization and a sprung bolster before fixing it. The owner’s example rendering does not select one of those architectures.
Spring and damper specification still required
Specify verified loaded and unloaded force/height points; rate tolerance; free, installed and solid heights; working OD and ID through deflection; end seating; material/process, corrosion protection and fatigue life; lateral-force and buckling behavior; maximum travel and retention. Obtain a damper force/velocity curve, compressed and extended eye dimensions, angular limits and rated end loads. Do not use a spring’s solid-height load as an operating rating.
Primary references, checked 19 October 2026: SKF · CFD · Lee Spring. These support the architecture and terminology; no OEM component dimensions or ratings have been transferred to the train.

NEXT ENGINEERING UNIT · THE COMPLETE COACH TRUCK
Design the suspension, wheelsets and brakes together.
Two four-wheel trucks per coach remain the development arrangement. The axle rotates in bearings inside nonrotating axleboxes. Suspension and guides connect those axleboxes to the truck frame; a separate bearing/pivot connection lets the complete truck turn beneath the coach.
Current status: the G1 model only depicts suspension and pivot functions. Its display spring, axlebox, wheel and frame dimensions are not selected hardware. No complete truck has yet been defined for fabrication.
Initial architecture to develop: guided axleboxes with coil primary springs, appropriate damping and bump/rebound retention, a fabricated truck frame and crossmember, and an engineered body center-bearing/pivot with controlled side bearings. Compare equalized primary suspension and a separately sprung bolster before fixing the arrangement. Spring counts, rates, travel and all mounting sizes remain engineering decisions.
Primary suspension permits wheelset motion relative to the truck. Truck pivoting permits yaw under the coach. Both need physical clearance and load analysis; a0% longitudinal grade does not remove crosslevel variation or track twist.
Complete-truck work that must be coordinated
Wheel/rail geometry, wheelbase, axle seats, bearings, housings, seals and retention.
Spring seats, empty/loaded rates and heights, travel, guidance, damping and stops; wheel-load behavior on track twist.
Frame members, crossmembers, joints, welds, machining datums and inspection access.
Body bolster, center bearing, retained pivot, side bearings, roll control and turning clearance.
Actual drum/hub, spider, cam, adjuster, chamber and carrier geometry throughout suspension travel.
A defined brake-reaction path that does not bind or overconstrain axlebox motion; hose movement and guards.
Assembly, wheelset removal, lifting, maintenance and physical prototype tests.
Architecture references: SKF axlebox explanation and CFD complete passenger bogie example. They explain real arrangements; neither supplies a verified36-inch-gauge design for this train. The proposed simpler primary-sprung architecture is a project engineering direction, not an OEM approval.
DRAWING QUALITY CORRECTION · Q1 · 18 OCTOBER 2026 PACIFIC
These brake sheets cannot be used to fabricate the parts.
The current three sheets are schematic layout and load-path studies. Their blocks explain proposed relationships; they do not describe complete physical components. Labels alone cannot make them buildable. The earlier presentation overstated their usefulness as assembly and fabrication drawings.
What is retained: the documented brake-family research, calculations and original studies. What is still missing: the actual custom part geometry and manufacturing definition. No additional mechanical design or fabrication approval is claimed by this presentation correction.

Required content for the next fabrication-development issue
 | Requirement
 | Project drawing acceptance criteria
 | Recognizable assembly
 | Show an assembled isometric or cutaway and its position on the bogie/car. Depict the actual selected mechanism and identify omitted parts. A rectangular placeholder is not a component model.
 | Part identification
 | Use consistent part numbers and numbered leaders tied to a bill of materials, with quantity, make/buy status, material or exact purchased part, and revision.
 | Manufacturable geometry
 | Give coordinated orthographic views, identified sections and enlarged details, units, scale or NOT TO SCALE, datums and all dimensions needed to make and inspect each part. Do not measure pixels.
 | Connections
 | Show each mating face, pilot, bolt pattern, fastener, thread, retention feature and assembly access. State supported fits, tolerances, torque or preload, and material requirements.
 | Welded assemblies
 | Show exact member and plate sizes, joint preparations, supported weld symbols/specifications, fabrication sequence, distortion control and inspection locations. Do not invent an unverified weld size.
 | Installation and service
 | Show real clearances, suspension and turning travel, guards, hose/cable routing, access and removal path. A proposed removal arrow does not establish that parts can be removed.
 | Engineering traceability
 | Link selections and critical dimensions to calculations, controlled manufacturer interfaces or explicit engineering decisions. Separate dimension closure from professional approval and physical test evidence.
 | Release status
 | Keep concept, engineering-review and fabrication-release status distinct. A complete-looking drawing with critical HOLDs is not a fabrication drawing ready for use. Record the responsible review and release evidence before marking it released.
A new schematic or rendering does not satisfy these criteria. The next issue must close a specific part or connection definition, or document the exact missing input and advance an independent part that can be developed. Drawings and rotatable models must agree and disclose simplified geometry.
Next work: establish the actual selected brake and wheel-end interfaces, then develop the hub and stationary support as individual parts with assembly details. If a critical supplier interface remains unavailable, identify that exact input and work on an independent, sufficiently defined train component.
SELECTED FOR PROTOTYPE DEVELOPMENT
Eight brake assemblies per coach.
Two per axle; thirty-two across four coaches. The catalog establishes a related commercial component family. The custom rail attachment and complete pressure-to-wheel torque match still require engineering and tests. The locomotive brake package remains a separate coordinated selection.

The custom Tolomatic linkage is no longer the lead.
Its drawings remain as a previous alternative. The new route uses the drum family's documented cam, automatic adjuster and combination chamber. The compact WABCO PAN17 air-disc family remains the fallback.
The decision is made for development; fabrication release is held.
The key remaining tests are low-force control when empty and loaded, reverse operation, parking-spring output, and the complete mounting and extraction envelope. Wheel diameter, axle-end joint, carrier material/thickness, drum pilot/bolt circle and welds are not production dimensions in this issue.

Engineering recordSelection, reasons and change ledger
3L.2 — Coach brake selection and mounting development
Issued 18 October 2026 Pacific / 19 October UTC. Preliminary engineering development. No fabrication, purchase or passenger-operation release.
Decision
Use the Dexter 12¼ × 4-inch PQ S-cam drum family with its documented Type 20/24 combination service/spring-parking chamber as the preferred coach prototype basis. Develop two brake assemblies per rotating axle: eight per four-axle coach, thirty-two across the three regular and one accessible coach. One brake per wheel is the prototype choice for paired wheel-end modules and separate reaction paths; the demand calculation does not prove that two per axle are necessary, and it does not establish a redundancy rating. The locomotive remains a separately matched foundation-brake package coordinated with the same train controls; its driven axle arrangement is not frozen.
This is a selected development route, not a completed rail application match. The OEM catalog establishes a coherent drum, shoe, spider, S-cam, adjuster and chamber family. It does not establish our rail axle, carrier, low-speed torque control, reverse performance, final mounting geometry or operating approval.
Why this route leads
The drum family uses the familiar air-service and spring-parking arrangement the owner requested. It has documented replaceable wear parts and removes the separate force-reduction linkage needed by the previous Tolomatic study. Its OEM 10K road-axle family provides a coherent compact-friction-diameter candidate. PAN17 has a higher published road application range, but neither rating establishes rail suitability or which complete installation is smaller, lighter or cheaper. Total installed dimensions, net masses and costs remain unresolved.
The proposed duty is low speed on a 0% longitudinal-grade target. Calculated energy per stop does not establish a thermal need for a highway-sized disc package. Both candidates still require cold, hot, repeated-stop and drag-fault verification. Low average cycle power is not evidence of acceptable friction temperature.
The compact WABCO PAN17 is retained as the fallback. Hendrickson documents a real PAN17/rotor/chamber package, but its publications contain application-dependent chamber variants and the retrieved generic caliper outline differs from the Hendrickson rotor diameter. Exact assembly revision must be reconciled before using its dimensions. The large Bendix ADB22X photo was an explanation of air-disc operation, not a component selection for this train.
What changes in the rail wheelset
The preferred transverse order is rail wheel → axle bearing/axlebox → outboard drum and removable hub. Both rail wheels stay fixed to a common rotating axle. The drum and its custom hub rotate with the axle. The shoe spider, cam supports and chamber bracket remain stationary and move with the axlebox through primary suspension travel. An engineered reaction path transfers their torque to the bogie without preventing suspension movement.
Retain the original drum mounting-face orientation. The Dexter drum is mounted inboard of its normal hub flange; a new flange placed against the opposite face would change that interface. Accordingly the service concept removes the custom axle-end hub and drum as a unit, then separates them on the bench. It does not claim the drum alone can slide past an obstructing retained hub flange.
The axle-end hub needs a positively retained, engineer-designed torque connection. Its spline, fit or other coupling geometry, axial retainer and fatigue details are held. No welded-on hub, improvised key, friction-only clamp or change to a rail-wheel press fit is specified. The outboard brake avoids inserting drum width between the rail wheel and its bearing, but adds outer width, overhung brake mass and mounting loads. These need a full bogie/frame/swept-envelope check.
Defined and deliberately unresolved
The 12¼-inch drum designation is the inside friction diameter, 311.15 mm; the 4-inch designation is nominal lining width, 101.6 mm. Neither is a complete outside envelope. The 500 and 600 mm rail-wheel diameters remain packaging studies. There is no calculated drum-to-rail clearance until the actual drum outside diameter, wear allowance, guards, clearances and running geometry are established.
The retrieved online drum listing mixes hub/wheel-related dimensions with the drum listing. The actual drum pilot and bolt circle are therefore not machining inputs in this issue. Obtain a controlled dimensional drawing or measure a traceable sample against it. Do not copy wheel-stud pitch into a drum flange.
At the upper regular-coach allocation, 600 mm wheel diameter, 0.7 m/s² and the stated rotational allowance, demand is approximately 364 N·m per brake for two brakes per axle. This is a demand, not measured brake output or carrier capacity. The chamber size designation is not a constant-force curve. No regulator pressure, spring-force value or parking-torque rating is invented from it.
Pneumatic consequence of the selected count
Thirty-two coach combination chambers change the air-demand basis. Recalculate service/emergency reservoir usable capacity, compressor recharge duty, line/valve flow, simultaneous apply/release timing and spring-release capacity, including the separately selected locomotive chambers. No earlier reservoir or compressor sizing is accepted merely because its part remains in the register. The catalog service and spring volumes at the stated reference condition are carried into the air-demand worksheet; they are not silently treated as standard free-air consumption or summed with an unknown locomotive count.
Conditions that decide whether D1 survives prototype development
Demonstrate controllable torque from release through normal service on both empty-allocation and fully loaded cases, without excessive initial grab, drag or wheel slide. Verify both direction sensitivities and cold/wet/seasonal conditions. Obtain torque–pressure–stroke–wear data and exact chamber spring/service curves.
Resolve the manufacturer's S-cam rotation guidance for reverse operation. Reverse running and reversing maneuvers must be evaluated; a forward-only installation is not accepted silently.
Complete the actual drum/hub, axle, carrier, bearing and chamber envelopes, including extraction, full suspension travel and curves. Retain original wear-part interfaces and positively retain every rotating part.
Confirm full-pressure, parking-spring, simultaneous-force/failure, jam and fatigue loads. Demand torque alone cannot size the carrier, hub joint, bolts or welds.
Verify the retained train service/emergency/parking architecture and test both separated portions. Spring application while moving is not automatically an acceptable emergency stop on steel rail. Retain anti-compounding and supervised parking behavior; finish the exact valve logic and failure assessment.
If the drum cannot meet the modulation, reverse or service-envelope gates, move to the documented compact air-disc branch and rework its carrier. Do not make the drum fit by altering shoes, cams, linings or the sealed spring chamber.
Fabrication development scope
Custom parts: rail wheels and axle, axle-end drum hubs, axleboxes, stationary spider carriers, cam/chamber support assemblies, articulated reaction links, guards and their bogie attachments. Standard replaceable brake internals and sealed air/spring chambers retain their manufacturer's identity. Farm-crew work begins with a non-load-bearing fit mockup and designated assembly tasks after drawings are released. Wheel/axle machining, heat treatment, fits, NDT, critical joints and brake performance validation require qualified specialists.
This issue supersedes the Tolomatic ME220/chamber linkage as the lead coach-brake route. Its earlier calculations and drawings are preserved as an alternative study and must not be mixed into the new drum assembly.Drum component evidence and reverse-operation gate
D1 — compact S-cam drum development basis
Issue 3L.2-DRUM-R0 · researched 19 October 2026 UTC / 18 October Pacific. Preliminary component-family selection for custom coaches; no fabrication release or rail performance approval.
Recommendation
Use the Dexter PQ 12¼ × 4 inch drum family with its specified Type 20/24 combination chamber as the preferred coach prototype basis: two brakes per rotating axle, eight per four-axle coach. Retain the commercial friction and actuation assembly; engineer the custom wheelset, axle-end drum hub, stationary spider carrier, chamber supports and torque reaction. This is a documented manufacturer family match, not yet a validated pressure-to-wheel torque match.
This is a more coherent starting point than combining an arbitrary 30/30 chamber with the previously illustrated industrial mechanical caliper. It preserves the user's air-service/spring-parking preference. A matched compact air-disc alternative remains a useful fallback if the drum's control or mounting limitations cannot be resolved. The locomotive needs its own driven-axle review; this coach choice is not automatically its brake choice.
Documented component family
Dexter's current resource page links its 2019.09 medium-duty catalog. Pages 12, 19 and 20 identify these related parts. D01 ↗, D02 ↗.
 | Component
 | OEM reference

 | 009-027-01; ABS version 009-027-03

 | Handed S-cams
 | 034-188-xx / 034-189-xx

 | 034-261-00, Type 20/24

 | 034-048-00
 | Drum screws
 | 007-244-00
 | Original trailer hub, reference only
 | 008-214-05
The 10K designation describes a highway axle family. It does not certify the proposed rail axle, braking torque, or custom mounting. The rail study's 3,300 kg axle load is not sufficient evidence of compatibility.
Dexter's currently linked 2018.11 heavy-duty catalog identifies the same 055-040-99 part as an angled automatic adjuster with a 6-inch arm and supplied clevis/setup kit. That is useful part evidence, but the exact delivered adjuster geometry, selected hole and 10K installation still require confirmation. The short 034-188-10/034-189-10 cam option is a packaging candidate only, not selected. D07 ↗.
The rail adaptation is substantial
The commercial trailer's axle beam and brake spider remain stationary while its wheel hub rotates on bearings. A conventional rail wheelset instead has two wheels fixed to a rotating axle. Therefore the OEM spider cannot be welded to this axle. The custom nonrotating brake support must be located from the axlebox/bearing housing so that the shoes stay concentric with the drum throughout primary suspension movement. Its torque reaction must reach the bogie without binding the suspension.
The preferred service layout is, moving outward: rail wheel → bearing/axlebox → drum with open mouth inward → removable rotating axle-end hub. The stationary spider is supported from the outboard axlebox face. This is an engineering concept, not a proven package.
The OEM exploded illustration shows the hub flange outside the drum's closed face. Preserve this support orientation in the development study and withdraw the drum and removable custom hub together as a service cartridge. Do not claim the drum alone simply slides over the hub flange. The hub-to-axle torque connection, positive axial retention, release tooling, bore clearance, fastener access and extraction space still need engineering and a physical fit check. A permanent interference-fit hub could defeat the intended service procedure; it has not been selected.
An alternative hub flange behind the drum face may permit a different extraction path, but would change the clamped joint and support arrangement. That alternative needs separate analysis. An inboard or center-axle drum could become trapped between pressed rail wheels and is not the preferred maintenance arrangement.
Size and mass evidence
12¼ inches is the nominal friction diameter, 311.15 mm. Four inches is the nominal lining width, 101.6 mm. Neither number establishes the drum's outside diameter or total axial depth. Actual OD, fins, spider protrusion, canister envelope, wear travel and guards remain in the clearance model as unresolved inputs.
The current Dexter drum listing confirms the separate drum and hub, lists eight mounting screws and a 51.7 lb package weight. Package weight is not a verified installed mass. Its published 8-on-6.5-inch and 4.75-inch pilot fields appear in associated hub information too, so their relevance to the drum mounting interface is uncertain. Obtain a controlled drawing and check the actual part before cutting a hub flange. No drum bolt circle or pilot diameter is released here. Inventory is behind dealer/login access; the listing does not confirm stock. D03 ↗.
Demand calculation — not brake output
Assume a 3,300 kg loaded regular-coach axle, wheel radius 0.25–0.30 m, zero longitudinal grade and an illustrative 5% rotational allowance. Required total axle torque is:
T_axle = mass × deceleration × rolling radius × 1.05
 | Deceleration
 | Wheel radius
 | One brake carrying axle demand
 | Each of two balanced brakes
 | 0.5 m/s²
 | 0.25 m
 | 433.125 Nm
 | 216.563 Nm
 | 0.5 m/s²
 | 0.30 m
 | 519.750 Nm
 | 259.875 Nm
 | 0.7 m/s²
 | 0.25 m
 | 606.375 Nm
 | 303.188 Nm
 | 0.7 m/s²
 | 0.30 m
 | 727.650 Nm
 | 363.825 Nm
At 7.5 mph, kinetic energy allocated to this axle is 19.475 kJ including that rotational allowance, or 9.738 kJ per brake with equal sharing. These are energy demands, not thermal ratings. They do not determine temperature or fade without the actual drum mass/material, duty cycle and heat-transfer data.
Two brakes halve nominal torque and heat per unit and allow separate service components. They add mass and expense and do not double rail adhesion: both act on the same rigid wheelset. One failed brake is not automatically covered by the other; the surviving brake and controls must be assessed for that fault. One brake per axle reduces component count but places all axle braking in one unit and can complicate drum extraction.
Performance gates before this becomes a final match
Obtain the exact chamber's service force versus pressure and stroke, and spring force versus stroke, with release pressure and travel limits. Type 20 or 24 is not a constant effective piston area at every stroke.
Obtain drum torque versus chamber pressure/S-cam input for the specified lining, drum, adjuster and setup, including low pressure, new/burnished/worn and temperature conditions. The small demanded torque makes minimum controllable braking important. Empty-coach wheel locking must be assessed alongside maximum loaded stopping demand.
Analyze unrestricted spring application and combined service/parking faults. Reducing service pressure does not reduce the mechanical parking spring's available force. Anti-compounding and spring emergency behavior belong to the train control design.
Confirm adequate reverse operation. Dexter's manual cautions that installing cam rotation opposite wheel rotation can cause chatter and wheel hop. The normal forward installation therefore does not by itself establish repeated bidirectional train performance. Request manufacturer/application assessment when authorized and include reverse tests.
Complete custom carrier, hub, axle-end, bearing and fatigue calculations with full actuator/fault torque. Do not use tire or rail slip as a structural overload limiter. Establish attachment details, tolerances, retention, guards, service access and inspection.
Bench-map the complete brake and then test an unoccupied prototype on controlled track, including empty/loaded cases, both directions, wet/contaminated rail, breakaway, power loss, wear and hot conditions. No such physical testing has occurred.
Dexter's service manual provides brake setup, stroke inspection and burnishing procedures and cautions against mixing superficially similar shoe components. Its highway FMVSS discussion is not an applicability decision for this train. D04 ↗.
Alternatives retained
The Dexter 12¼ × 5 family uses the larger Type 24/30 combination chamber and has wider friction hardware. There is no demonstrated need to enlarge the current compact candidate. Hendrickson's HXS 12.25 × 7.5 H20 family, including shoe kit HK4692HXH20, is another documented commercial option but wider again. Its complete axle-specific configuration would need to be recovered before substitution. Hendrickson explicitly distinguishes its shoe and cam geometry from other 12.25-inch systems; matching drum diameter or a lining number does not establish interchangeability. D05 ↗, D06 ↗.
Source PDFs and rendered OEM pages in the research folder are internal evidence only. Publish the original report/data and links, not rehosted manufacturer documents. No orders, emails, supplier assurances, professional approvals or physical tests are represented by this issue.Compact disc comparator and exact revision holds
Compact air-disc component match — 3L.2 development evidence R0
The strongest documented compact disc candidate is the WABCO PAN17 / Hendrickson 325 × 34 mm package. It is a real direct-mounted air-caliper system, avoiding the unproven semi-chamber-to-Tolomatic external lever. It is a comparator to the drum option, not a rail-approved purchase selection. Physical interfaces now supported
ZF's exact 40175073 listing identifies a six-point M14 × 1.5 mounting interface and a 20.082 kg caliper without pads. Its linked OEM outline gives useful actual reference geometry: R216 maximum shown caliper radial profile, chamber axis 191.5 mm from the axle, and a 330 mm reference disc. The linked 20/24 chamber family drawing explicitly includes 9253800250/0240 and supplies a 185.5 mm body, 64 mm stroke, short R8 ball-ended rod, mounting/vent orientation, and force-versus-stroke curves. The curves are labelled informational, not guaranteed acceptance curves. These are replacement-product data, not the native Hendrickson controlled production drawing; final rotor ID and drawing must agree before hub machining. D10 ↗.

Rotor-edge height above rail is (railwheel diameter − rotor diameter)/2; it is not a minimum permitted running clearance.

The caliper and chamber extend beyond the rotor, and their clocking changes downward projection. Top-mounted versus side-mounted packaging must include the floor, primary suspension, guide movement, wheel wear, guard rails and hoses. Hendrickson's returned legacy L1272 guide calls for approximately25.4mm inward movement allowance as pads/rotor wear; actual full-wear CAD governs. The landing page labels Rev B2023 while its download returns a2016 Rev- file; that revision mismatch remains logged. D08 ↗, D09 ↗.
At the project upper regular-coach axle mass3300kg,0.7m/s² deceleration,0.30m rolling radius and5% rotational allowance, total axle demand is727.65Nm. Two equal wheel brakes would each contribute363.825Nm; one axle-mounted brake would carry727.65Nm. These are required operating torques, not structural maximums. The commercial brake's available torque may be much greater: controlled low-force modulation, tare/load balance and uncontrolled spring application are selection gates. A nominal chamber area × pressure estimate cannot close that match.
Custom fabrication decision
If the disc route wins, retain the complete purchased caliper, its integral adjuster, matched chamber, specified pads and replaceable rotor. Engineer a custom rotor hub and a nonrotating carrier referenced to the axleboxes so the brake follows primary suspension motion; transmit reaction into the bogie through a designed torque path. A rotating rail axle cannot directly inherit a truck's stationary spindle/axle housing arrangement. Carrier material, thickness, bolts, joints, fit and fatigue release remain held.
PAN17 is the preferred disc comparator because it is substantially smaller than the430mm ADB22X shown as an explanation. PAN19's375mm rotor offers no demonstrated need here. Meritor ELSA175 is another330mm family, but an exact matching chamber was not established and its manufacturer's listed availability regions excludeNorthAmerica. No present stock, price or service turnaround is claimed. D11 ↗, D12 ↗.
The next decisive evidence is a complete current part configuration, certified low-input service/parking transfer curves, exact installed envelope and a comparable drum package. Until those close, neither disc nor drum is a fabrication-release choice.Traceable demand, energy, stopping and adhesion calculations
Brake selection demand basis — 3L.2 R0
Issue: 18 October 2026, America/Los_Angeles. Preliminary comparison for custom fabrication development; no component is selected or construction approved by these calculations.
What is carried forward
The source mass table in the saved Phase 3K technical package has been inspected. Three regular coaches at 13,200 kg, one accessible coach at 11,000 kg, and a 14,500 kg locomotive/hotel allocation sum to 65,100 kg. These are analytical upper allocations, not measured weights or ratings. The verified predecessor also contains 7,040 kg regular-coach and 7,535 kg accessible-coach tare allocations. Their empty-train sum is 43,155 kg, retaining the same locomotive allocation; this is an allocation-empty comparison, not a weighed empty consist. Actual crew, consumables and corner weights remain to be established.
Four equally loaded and equally braked axles per coach, wheel diameters of 500 and 600 mm, decelerations of 0.5 and 0.7 m/s², speeds of 5 and 7.5 mph, and one versus two friction-brake assemblies per axle are comparison assumptions. The locomotive brake count remains unknown and has not been filled in.
Mechanics and the 5% rotation sensitivity
For a vehicle of mass m at wheel radius r, on level track:
Rail contact force for prescribed translational deceleration: F_contact = m a.
Brake torque summed at wheel radius: sum(T/r) = m(1+k)a.
Kinetic energy assigned to the friction brakes: E = ½ m(1+k)v².
The upper-load sensitivity uses k=0.05, representing sum(I/r²)=0.05m_upper; actual wheel, axle, brake and drivetrain inertias are unknown. That absolute equivalent inertia is held fixed when passengers are removed, so k is higher in the allocation-empty cases.
The rotation allowance increases the required brake torque and dissipated energy. It does not multiply the whole-train external rail force for a prescribed deceleration. Internal per-axle distributions require the actual inertia and brake allocation. No engine brake, regenerative recovery, rolling resistance or aerodynamic drag is credited.
Torque demanded of each friction-brake assembly
Values below use an equivalent rotational mass equal to 5% of each vehicle's upper mass allocation, held fixed between upper and allocation-empty cases. Two brakes per axle ideally split the axle demand in half; this is not evidence that a failed unit can be ignored or that both brakes share perfectly.

For the upper train at 0.5 m/s², rail-contact force is 32.55 kN, while the 5%-rotation brake-torque equivalent is 34.18 kN. At 0.7 m/s² they are 45.57 kN and 47.85 kN. A catalog brake capable of far more torque is not automatically unsuitable, but minimum controllable torque, load compensation and wheel-slip behavior must be demonstrated. No force-to-pressure map or chamber size is selected here.
Energy and repeated-stop duty
Each row uses the 5%-of-upper-mass rotation sensitivity, with that absolute equivalent inertia retained in allocation-empty cases, and one friction brake per axle (four per coach). With two brakes per axle, the ideal energy and mean thermal input per brake are half the row values. Energy is independent of wheel diameter under this lumped model; the actual choice changes inertia and must later replace k. These values are heat input, not brake temperatures or thermal ratings.

The 65,100 kg train carries 170.75 kJ at 5 mph and 384.20 kJ at 7.5 mph, including the 5% rotation sensitivity. A loaded regular coach at 7.5 mph and 0.7 m/s² initially puts about 8.13 kW into each of its four brakes, despite its much smaller cycle-average heat input. Actual fade/cooling checks need drum/rotor and lining data, enclosure airflow, ambient conditions, consecutive stop patterns, drag faults and supplier duty acceptance. Low mean power alone cannot qualify a brake.
Illustrative stops and adhesion
The following is constant deceleration after 2 seconds with zero braking, an illustrative command-to-effective-braking delay. Driver recognition/reaction is additional; actual air propagation and build-up must be measured. These distances are not acceptance limits or operating-speed approvals.

For ideal all-weight-braked operation on level track, µ_required=a/g: 0.05099 at 0.5 m/s² and 0.07138 at 0.7 m/s². These are required coefficients under assumptions, not claims about available rail adhesion. Axle unloading, unequal brake effort, wet/contaminated rail and unbraked weight can govern earlier. No minimum available adhesion is established.

At µ=0.05, the ideal ceiling is 0.490 m/s²: neither the 0.5 nor 0.7 m/s² comparison is guaranteed. At µ=0.08, the ideal ceiling is 0.785 m/s² before unequal loads and distribution. Merely fitting a stronger brake does not remove this limit.
Applying the same loaded-command torque to the tare allocation, without load compensation, gives:

The regular allocation-empty vehicle is especially sensitive: the same loaded-command torque produces 1.80 times the upper-load deceleration under the fixed-equivalent-inertia sensitivity (660 kg equivalent rotational mass retained). The simple translational-mass ratio alone is 1.875; it is not used for this fixed-wheelset calculation. Pressure/force control, reliable load compensation and wheel-slip behavior therefore matter for either a drum or disc design. Available stroke and spring-parking behavior must also be checked at minimum load; an air regulator does not limit mechanical spring force.
Parking and off-design stress cases
The owner's alignment remains 0% grade. At exactly zero grade with no external disturbance, the gravity-only holding calculation gives zero torque; that does not constitute a meaningful parking-brake qualification. Wind, coupling force, surveyed grade tolerance, degraded brakes, hold duration, release detection and an accepted margin still need a defined test basis.
The following stress cases illustrate residual 0.5% and 1% grade only; they do not change the route requirement. Values are per brake for a 600 mm wheel and one brake per axle, without a safety factor or favorable rolling resistance. Two brakes per axle halve the ideal share. No spring chamber is matched by these values alone.
 | Coach upper allocation
 | 0.5% stress grade
 | 1% stress grade
 | regular upper
 | 48.54 Nm
 | 97.08 Nm
 | accessible upper
 | 40.45 Nm
 | 80.90 Nm
On a downhill stress grade theta=atan(grade fraction), use F_contact=m(a+g sin(theta)); required torque adds m g sin(theta) r to the translational-and-rotational deceleration torque. Required adhesion becomes [a/g+sin(theta)]/cos(theta). Parking is a static problem and has no 5% kinetic-rotation multiplier.
Implications for selecting the hardware
The largest regular-coach comparison is 727.65 Nm per axle at 600 mm wheel diameter, 0.7 m/s² and k=0.05; two friction brakes per axle would each carry 363.83 Nm under ideal sharing. That is demanded working torque, not the mounting design load. Bracket, axle interface, drum/rotor attachment and suspension torque reaction must survive the selected hardware's maximum credible output and fault cases, including spring/service interaction, reverse motion, friction extremes, adjustment, jam and thermal effects. Demand calculations do not establish those ratings.
A matched commercial drum or air-disc assembly must be compared against the same required controllable torque, spring holding requirement, stroke/wear margin, wheel/axle packaging, empty-weight adhesion and actual duty. The recommended design cannot be justified merely by a component's much larger truck rating or by nominal chamber type.
Reproduction and evidence
calculate_brake_selection.py writes the input register, full numeric cases, this report and arithmetic checks. It reads the verified mass-basis member of the saved Phase 3K source ZIP; the member SHA-256 is recorded. The governing equations above are derived from translational/rotational equilibrium, kinetic energy, constant-deceleration kinematics and static slope balance; they are original calculation statements, not claimed manufacturer ratings. No external commercial specifications are introduced in this file. The checks confirm arithmetic and internal model consistency only. They do not replace professional review, component qualification or physical braking tests.Custom mounting and service details
Custom drum / wheelset integration — D1 development layout
The recommended layout to develop is rail wheel → main axle bearing / axlebox → outboard brake, with a separate commercial drum mounted on a custom removable axle-end hub. The drum and hub withdraw together outward for service. The pressed rail wheel and main axle bearing can remain installed if the detailed hub joint and retention arrangement preserve that access. This is a design requirement, not a demonstrated fit or an approved wheel end.
The lead foundation family is Dexter 12¼ × 4 inch S-cam with its catalog-associated Type 20 service / 20-24 combination spring chamber. The drawings use the nominal 311.15 mm friction inside diameter and 101.6 mm lining width only. Neither is the outside package size. Wheel diameters of 500 and 600 mm remain study cases. A 914.4 mm track-gauge reference does not specify wheel back-to-back, wheel width, axle length, bearing position, or curve clearance.
Why the removable hub matters
Dexter’s medium-duty catalog, printed page 12, shows drum 009-027-01 inboard of the hub flange. The drum mouth faces inboard toward the spider; the closed face seats against the hub flange on its outboard side. The drum mounting screws are depicted entering from the drum side. The drawings retain this seating orientation.
Putting a permanent hub flange on the inside of the drum’s closed face would allow a superficially attractive outward drum slide, but would change the supported mounting face and joint stack. That is not a verified use of this drum. This proposal therefore removes the hub and drum as one unit, then separates them on a bench with access to the inboard screws. It does not require the drum bore to pass over a retained, larger hub flange.
The custom axle-end hub must transmit reversing brake torque through an engineered removable joint, with a separate engineered axial-retention provision. Joint type, engagement, fits, axle transitions, material, heat treatment, runout, retention and tools remain held. A key, taper, spline, or interference fit is not selected by these illustrations. The original trailer hub is not adopted as a rail hub. Its bearing system is arranged around a stationary road spindle; our rail axle rotates in its own main bearings.
Stationary carrier and load path
The spider and its cam / chamber support belong to a nonrotating carrier referenced to the axlebox. This assembly follows primary suspension movement with the axle. An articulated reaction arm / link transfers brake torque to the sprung bogie. A carrier clamped or welded directly onto the rotating rail axle would be mechanically wrong.
The link must have an actual perpendicular moment arm about the axle axis. The plan projection on L2-M03 cannot establish that arm by itself; the end-view inset shows the required torque plane and the relationship F = T / h. Both tension and compression, joint angular travel, suspension motion and any lateral axle movement require analysis. The link must not overconstrain the axlebox or become a suspension stop.
The rotating force path is rail wheel → axle → removable hub joint → drum. The reaction path is shoes → spider → carrier → reaction link → bogie. Chamber anchor force, slack-adjuster/cam force, cam-support bending, brake torque, component weight and vibration must be combined. The outboard brake position preserves the opportunity for a short wheel-to-bearing span, but adds outboard rotating and stationary masses and their cantilever effects.
For the current calculation study, a 13,200 kg regular coach with four braked axles and two brakes per axle, 600 mm wheels, 0.7 m/s² deceleration and a 5% inertia allowance requires 363.825 N·m per brake under ideal equal allocation. This is a working demand, not a structural design maximum. Hub and carrier sizing must also cover the manufacturer’s maximum credible brake output, parking-spring force, control faults, reverse operation and combined loads. A low normal brake-pressure setting does not establish the maximum mechanical load.
Layout alternatives
 | Arrangement
 | Service consequence
 | Development position
 | Wheel → bearing → outboard drum and removable hub
 | Hub/drum can withdraw outward while wheel and main bearing stay; bearing retention and extraction tools must be coordinated
 | Preferred conditional layout
 | Wheel → drum → outboard bearing
 | Drum is trapped behind bearing/spider unless those are removed first; can increase wheel-to-bearing spacing
 | Fallback if the preferred outer envelope cannot fit
 | Drum between the two pressed rail wheels
 | Ordinary axial drum removal may be trapped by wheels; split or special interfaces would require separate engineering
 | Not the lead layout
 | Rail wheel itself used as friction drum
 | Combines rail-wheel strength, thermal effects and brake wear; replacement and qualification become coupled
 | Rejected as the starting arrangement
The preferred layout is not proven to fit beneath the current 110 inch coach body. Drum outside diameter/depth, actual wheel profile, bearing dimensions, camshaft sweep, adjuster and chamber stroke, guards, suspension travel, bogie rotation, stairs, track clearance and service space are all required in a common 3D envelope.
Custom fabrication interfaces to complete
Axle-end hub: establish axle-axis datum A, engineered hub seating/retention datum B and OEM drum seating datum C. Obtain the controlled drum pilot and bolt pattern; the available catalog metadata is ambiguous and must not be transferred to a cut drawing. Prove joint fatigue, runout, reversing torque and repeatable removal.
Carrier / spider seat: preserve OEM spider geometry and cam axis. Determine whether an OEM-approved welded spider seat, another authorized attachment, or a purpose-designed foundation support is required. Record material and weldability before selecting a welding procedure. Do not infer a bolt pattern on a spider intended for welding to a stationary road axle.
Cam / chamber support: match the exact Type 20/24 chamber, automatic slack adjuster, camshaft handedness, brackets and operating travel. A different chamber with the same nominal size is not automatically interchangeable.
Reaction arm / link: establish h and 3D joint positions from combined motion and load cases; then size the members, pins, bearings and attachments. Reverse wheel rotation and the OEM’s S-cam rotation warning require a deliberate bidirectional operating review.
Service and guarding: provide a straight withdrawal path for the hub/drum unit, lifting / pulling provisions, inboard screw access on the bench, clearance to hoses and full protection from rotating components.
A practical next physical check is a full-size, non-load-bearing fit fixture made from controlled OEM component envelopes or measured actual parts. It should demonstrate shoe access, cam/adjuster travel, hose motion and extraction without moving the pressed rail wheel. That fixture does not qualify the train brake or authorize passenger operation.
Source and drawing status
Primary geometry / arrangement source: Dexter Medium Duty 9,000–15,000 lbs Axle Capacity Catalog, 2019.09 ↗, printed pages 12, 19–20, reviewed 2026-10-19 UTC. The page 19 catalog-association establishes a component-family candidate; it is not a torque curve or approval for a rotating-axle passenger railway application. Current revision and availability must be confirmed before selection.
L2-M01 is the assembly section and nominal radial comparison. L2-M02 is the service explosion and assembly requirements. L2-M03 shows the carrier, reaction mechanism and custom-part release evidence. All are original SVG source drawings. Their unverified geometry is deliberately schematic; no hidden manufacturing dimensions may be scaled from them. No production cut, weld, press, drill or machining release is included in this issue.Prototype sequence and fabrication release evidence
D1 brake prototype and fabrication evidence
This is a development sequence, not an instruction to operate an unfinished passenger vehicle. No supplier has been contacted and no parts have been ordered.
Gate A — Freeze the commercial brake configuration
Resolve current revisions and supply status for the Dexter 10K 12¼ × 4-inch PQ wear set, 20/24 combination chamber, automatic adjuster, clevis and left/right S-cams. Obtain the controlled drum, spider, cam, adjuster and chamber drawings. Keep exact lining identity and permitted drum condition; similar-looking replacement parts are not automatically equivalent. Confirm the intended cam rotation for forward and reverse operation.
The drum pilot, flange pattern, thicknesses and axle-end hub joint are held. Record actual component part/revision, material traceability where provided, physical dimensions and individual net masses. The online package weight is not the installed drum mass. Do not machine a flange from product-category dimensions.
Gate B — Full-size fit and maintenance mockup
A farm crew can help build a non-load-bearing envelope mockup using the released study coordinates and actual samples when available. It must clearly carry a non-operating label. Check wheel, axlebox, drum/hub, spider, cam support, slack-adjuster travel, chamber body and pushrod, hose bends, lubrication and inspection access. Check all suspension extremes and the complete bogie swept envelope. Check wrench access and a straight extraction path for the complete hub/drum unit. The rail wheel and axle bearing should remain installed during that extraction.
A brake spider will not be welded to the rotating axle. The OEM stationary-beam weld detail cannot simply become a weld to a different material. Develop the stationary carrier's material, weldability, joint or adapter, WPS, distortion control, machining datums and inspection as one drawing set.
Gate C — Instrumented brake bench
Before final production carrier dimensions, test or obtain accepted manufacturer data for the exact brake configuration on a guarded, specialist-designed rig. Establish instrumentation range, calibration uncertainty, fixture rating and approved pressure/force limits before energizing it. Record applied service pressure, rod force/stroke, shaft torque and rotation direction, shoe clearance, lining/drum state, temperatures and release drag.
Measure the onset of braking and the controllable range through the coach demand band; include repeatability, hysteresis, release and full-wear adjustment conditions. At the current 600 mm wheel study and 0.7 m/s² case, each regular-coach brake needs approximately 202 N·m in the allocation-empty state and 364 N·m at the upper allocation. The accessible-coach comparison is approximately 212–303 N·m. Those targets are conditional demands, not approved test tolerances or hardware limits.
Test forward and reverse at the same conditions. If the exact S-cam installation produces unacceptable chatter, grab, wheel-hop tendency or direction asymmetry, revise the architecture or use the compact air-disc branch. Do not grind cams or alter friction materials to make an unapproved combination appear to pass.
Establish service and parking force over the entire permitted stroke, maximum spring force, spring release requirement, anti-compounding behavior, and maximum credible jam/fault output. A pressure regulator does not limit spring force. These outputs supply the structural load envelope for the axle-end joint, drum fasteners, spider, cam supports, chamber bracket and reaction links.
Gate D — Release custom hardware drawings
Complete the wheel/axle profile and material basis; axle-end torque connection and separate axial retention; bearing/axlebox support; drum hub pilot and flange; stationary carrier, spider interface and cam/chamber support; articulated torque-reaction path; guards and maintenance access. Each part drawing requires material grade/condition, datums, dimensions/tolerances, fits/surface finish, joint details, inspection and revision identity. Strength and fatigue checks include operating, maximum-actuator, reverse, combined and fault cases. Assembly instructions reference exact commercial manuals rather than replacing them with guessed torque settings.
Qualified wheel/axle and precision fabrication specialists perform critical machining, heat treatment, NDT, fits and joint fabrication. The farm crew can undertake released guards, finishes, mockups and designated installation/assembly work within the signed responsibility plan.
Gate E — One complete bogie, then the consist
Build and inspect one bogie before repeating parts. Check primary-suspension alignment, both brake reaction paths per axle, release clearance and service extraction. Follow with controlled empty and ballasted vehicle tests on an approved test track, then the complete train. Verify pressure propagation, stopping distance, low-adhesion response, load compensation, parking, breakaway/separation, power loss and recovery. Recalculate the pneumatic budgets for thirty-two coach combination chambers plus the separately matched locomotive: usable reservoir capacity, compressor recharge, line/valve flow, simultaneous application and release, and spring-release capacity. The locomotive must participate in the brake-balance model; coach brakes are not silently tasked with stopping an unbraked locomotive.
Use the planned operating stop cycle, ambient conditions and foreseeable drag/hold faults for thermal qualification. The five- and ten-minute calculation intervals are illustrative sensitivities. Engineers set acceptance values before testing; record results and failures instead of labeling a checklist as a passed test.
Fallback rule
D1 proceeds only if the component, modulation, reverse, mechanical-envelope and structural gates close. If it fails, use the WABCO PAN17/Hendrickson compact disc branch after closing its exact chamber/rotor/caliper revision chain. Keep the remainder of the custom bogie and train-control interfaces coordinated. This is a predefined design decision, not a request for the owner to choose a brake type without evidence.Primary-source register · checked 19 October 2026 UTC

CUSTOM BUILD · WHOLE-TRAIN DRAWING REGISTER
Parts to fabricate. Assemblies to build.
The locomotive, coaches, bogies, wheelsets, mechanical brake supports, couplings and boarding structures are being developed as custom assemblies. Exact purchased engines, bearings, air chambers and wear parts will be defined in their mating drawings.
Whole sprung truck: custom geometry still in development
D1 drum-brake development supersedes the earlier caliper lead. The earlier wheelset/caliper drawings remain available as predecessor studies, not the current fabrication solution. Spring seats, guides and brake reaction need controlled axlebox and delivered brake interfaces before dimensions can be released.

 | WS — Custom wheelsets, axles and bearing housings
 | Wheel manufacturing geometry; Axle with wheel, bearing and brake-interface seats; Bearing housing and retention parts; Any separate rotor/hub carrier and mechanical torque-transfer interface
 | 36-inch gauge is only an evaluation basis. Wheel diameter, wheel/rail profile, back-to-back, axle seats and interference fits are not released.  | 3K documents candidate chamber geometry and conditional braking demand. It does not contain a selected mechanical brake or release-ready mount.  | Level longitudinal target is retained. Final alignment awaits survey and North Pole location.  | Typical interfaces can progress; station locations and final civil structures are not fixed. Vehicle swept envelope and loaded/empty floor movement; Boarding/accessibility solution; Survey, geotechnical and authority requirements; Drainage, utilities and responder accessDrawing contents, responsibilities and development sequence
Custom fabrication: the complete train
The design target is a complete custom-built train, with drawings that show how each part is made and how the parts connect. The next work must develop the wheelsets, bogies and mechanical brakes together. The existing brake diagrams explain control and forces, but do not yet define a manufacturable brake carrier, wheel, axle or bogie.
This direction applies to the locomotive, both coach types, running gear, couplings, platforms, stairs and installation interfaces. Engines, bearings, air chambers, valves and friction wear parts can be specified manufactured components within those custom assemblies. Their exact interfaces must appear in our drawings; a generic cylinder or an instruction to “fit suitable brakes” is not sufficient. A purchased complete bogie remains a benchmark or fallback, rather than the assumed solution.
Current status: Phases through 3K are desktop review packages. No assembly has a fabrication release, and no physical manufacturing or brake test is claimed. Earlier frame dimensions and CAD are useful development work; they do not establish that the complete train can already be fabricated from them.
Immediate package: 3L.1 — wheelset and brake carrier
The immediate deliverable is a small, coordinated assembly that a rail engineer and machine/fabrication shop can work through together:
Wheelset assembly: section through both wheels, axle, bearings, housings and retention; declared datums; rail/profile interface; wheel and axle detail sheets; bearing stack; brake torque-transfer interface.
Mechanical brake assembly: assembled side, plan and section views; exploded view; chamber, carrier, reaction structure, linkage or caliper, friction surface, adjustment, retention and guards. Show the load path into the bogie and the torque path into the wheelset.
Individual custom parts: carrier, chamber bracket, levers/pivots as applicable, rotor/hub adapter or tread-shoe supports, fasteners and welded joints. Every part has its own identifier and a corresponding BOM entry.
Fit and movement checks: actuator stroke, worn/new friction conditions, pad/shoe clearance, wheel and bearing removal, chamber tool access, suspension travel, yaw and full tolerance stack.
Manufacturing and inspection plan: raw material and process requirements, machining sequence, fixtures, first-article measurements, weld/NDT requirements and instrumented brake tests with acceptance values set by the responsible engineer.
The first dependency is the mechanical brake architecture and its actual component interfaces. Disc and tread arrangements cannot share one generic bracket drawing. Final wheel profile/back-to-back depends on the selected rail and turnout interface; final axle seats depend on the wheel, bearing and torque-transfer fits. Final chamber bracket and linkage depend on documented service and spring force versus travel, including wear adjustment. These unresolved interfaces will be called out by name, rather than hidden behind a completed-looking model.
The sequence is design basis → wheel/axle/bearing stack → brake carrier/linkage → controlled first article → complete bogie. Preliminary dimensions can support packaging and calculations before release. Safety-critical production dimensions, material conditions and acceptance values cannot be supplied merely to fill a blank.

What makes a part drawing ready for fabrication
The part number will identify the family, assembly and part, for example PF-WS-010-020 for an axle in the first wheelset assembly. Revision and actual manufactured serial number remain separate. Existing frame member identifiers will be retained and cross-referenced.
A released detail drawing must contain, or explicitly reference, the applicable items below. A justified “not applicable” is appropriate for processes the part does not need.
 | Drawing content
 | Required definition
 | Identity and geometry
 | Part/revision/parent/quantity/handedness, units, datums, controlling dimensions, sections and matching assembly references.
 | Material and process
 | Exact specification, grade, condition and product form; material certification; cut/blank and machining allowance; forming/casting/forging route where applicable.
 | Precision surfaces
 | Mating fits, runout/alignment/concentricity, surface finishes, holes/threads, heat treatment and hardness where needed.
 | Joints
 | Weld joint preparation, size/extent, approved WPS and distortion sequence; exact fasteners and reviewed locking/installation requirements.
 | Assembly and service
 | BOM/exploded references, fixture/alignment or press procedure, wear adjustment, removal access, edge treatment, coating and marking.
 | Acceptance
 | Measurable dimensions and limits, instruments/datums, hold/witness points, NDT and actual record forms; linked calculation/interface documents and authorized release.
Wheel/axle manufacturing drawings need a specialist manufacturing route and accepted material, fatigue, fit and inspection basis. The project should not silently convert a wheel model into a welded-plate wheel design. Similarly, a bracket outline becomes a fabrication detail only after its reaction forces, stiffness, fatigue, fasteners/welds, travel and inspection access have been resolved.
Who closes each step
The responsible vehicle/brake engineer accepts the loads, geometry, material conditions, fatigue design, joints and measurable acceptance limits. The fabricator establishes the capable manufacturing process and supplies actual conformity records. Wheel/axle machining, heat treatment, interference assembly and specified NDT require suitably capable specialists. Farm-crew assignments follow the released scope and demonstrated skills/equipment; preparation, fit mockups, finishing, designated assembly and supervised track work can be separated into clear work orders.
The drawing status will progress from development, to prototype released, to production released, and finally as-built accepted. Operational release is a separate commissioning gate. A prototype release applies only to its defined first article and test plan; a completed desktop phase does not authorize passenger use.
The existing sequence remains: finish 3L running gear, then 3M couplings, 3N propulsion, 3O bodies, 3P HVAC/electrical, 3Q boarding/accessibility, 3R track/stations and 3S coordinated release. Procurement and fabrication planning start with these drawing packages; phases 4–7 control actual purchasing, production, installation and commissioning. Work on independent assemblies continues while an interface is awaiting evidence.
Delivery: the technical site will carry the assembly drawings, part details, BOM, calculations, held decisions and change history. No new PDF is being saved to the conversation. This map changes the emphasis to custom fabrication; it does not add invented hardware ratings or claim an engineering approval.

Brake-carrier calculation and joint development
Custom brake carrier: the dimensions and loads now established
Revision PF-TRAIN-BRAKE-MECHANISM-MOUNT-R0 — October 18, 2026, Pacific time.
This develops the custom mounting interface around the Tolomatic ME220MEFI / 0745-0024 cast-iron mechanical caliper and a 12-inch fixed-disc packaging study. It preserves the requested air-service / spring-parking concept. The semi-type combination chamber, reduction mechanism, rotor, axle and finished carrier remain a coordinated selection; this is not a fabrication release.
Real interface dimensions
The manufacturer's floating-bracket drawing provides two 0.386-inch nominal holes on 4.750-inch centers: the dimensioned relative pattern is H1=(0,0), H2=(4.750,0) inches. With a 12-inch disc, its table places the lever axis 5.000 inches above the axle. The hole line is another 2.170 inches above the lever axis. For this calculation only, the hole pair is assumed horizontally centered on the axle/cam axis, giving hole centers (−2.375, 7.170) and (+2.375, 7.170) inches, or (−60.325, 182.118) and (+60.325, 182.118) mm. The image does not explicitly dimension that horizontal centering; controlled OEM CAD or physical-part measurement must establish it. These are conditional layout coordinates, not a released hole diameter or drill schedule for the custom support. The candidate accepts a 0.500-inch disc. [FM-02]
The rotor's effective friction radius is 5.080 inches / 129.032 mm. That is distinct from its 6-inch outside radius and from the lever-axis height. The caliper's 4-inch left lever arm is a possible linkage input location shown on the drawing; the manufacturer must confirm that the published input-force rating applies there and confirm permitted input direction. Do not attach a large chamber to the shorter arm by analogy.
Loads that size this mount
For the upper regular-coach allocation of 13,200 kg, four equally braked axles, level track and 0.7 m/s² deceleration, each axle supplies 2,310 N at the rail. At 0.300 m wheel radius, the axle demand is 693 Nm; a separate assumed 5% rotational allowance raises it to 727.65 Nm. The allowance is not a calculated wheelset inertia.
The OEM equations use dynamic torque = 7.45 × braking radius × lever force, and static torque = 3.725 × braking radius × lever force, with consistent inch/lbf units. The factor is a torque relationship, not a pad-clamping-force ratio. [FM-03]
 | Analysis case
 | Lever force
 | Axle brake torque
 | Peak in-plane load at one mounting hole*
 | Highest present stopping-demand probe
 | 170.17 lbf / 756.95 N
 | 727.65 Nm
 | 4.49 kN
 | 580 lbf aluminum-version comparison
 | 2,579.97 N
 | 2,480.09 Nm
 | 15.31 kN
 | 660 lbf cast-iron catalog input sensitivity
 | 2,935.83 N
 | 2,822.18 Nm
 | 17.42 kN
 | 660 lbf, static equation comparison
 | 2,935.83 N
 | 1,411.09 Nm
 | 10.32 kN
*Calculated rigid, in-plane two-hole interface model, conditional on the horizontally centered pair, including the lever input at the assumed 4-inch arm and the equivalent disc tangential force; opposite disc rotation and both input-force signs are enveloped. This is not bolt capacity. The linked parts instructions identify the aluminum version and its 580-lbf limit; the cast-iron catalog lists 660 lbf. Exact cast-iron installation instructions, the horizontal mounting datum and the rated input location remain to be obtained. Neither force value is permission to apply that load to an arbitrary linkage point.
The key consequence is concrete: sizing a bracket for the normal stopping demand alone would miss much larger actuator-driven reactions. The two mounting holes can see a calculated 17.42 kN reaction each in the catalog-force probe even though only 4.49 kN appears in the stopping-demand probe. Full-pressure, spring-parking, compounded and jam loads still have to bound that envelope. Rail slip is not a dependable structural overload limiter.
What connects to what
The caliper floating bracket bolts to a custom machined carrier. That carrier follows the wheelset through primary suspension travel, using an axlebox-referenced support or an engineered equivalent. A torque-reaction connection carries brake torque into the bogie while accommodating required movement. The wheel/axle/rotor turn inside that stationary support. The manufacturer’s axial floating bracket does not accommodate unrestricted vertical wheelset movement.
The two-hole calculation isolates the caliper. A complete carrier calculation also includes the chamber-anchor load and the reduction linkage pivots. If the chamber and caliper share one carrier, their opposed actuation reactions still load local brackets even when they partly cancel in the complete assembly. The ideal net pad-normal forces are not simply added to the mount as an external load; imbalance, misalignment and axial offsets need their own cases.
For example, a torque link with a hypothetical 200 mm perpendicular lever arm carries 3.64 kN at the present 727.65 Nm demand and 14.11 kN in the 660-lbf catalog torque probe. The actual perpendicular arm changes with linkage geometry and suspension travel. Those figures do not size a pin or select a bearing.
A force-reducing linkage also increases output travel. Both the chamber's force curves and the caliper's new/worn-pad travel must close together. A service-pressure regulator does not limit the parking spring force. Required ratio, stops, pins and wear adjustment therefore belong on one released mechanism drawing, not on separate guessed brackets.
Fabrication decisions and measurable records
 | Item
 | Information that the fabrication drawing must contain
 | Evidence needed to release it
 | Carrier plate and stiffeners
 | Material specification, thicknesses, radii, edge distances, machined datum faces and cut geometry
 | Full load envelope; bending, shear, bearing, tear-out, prying, local-support stiffness and fatigue analysis
 | Caliper joint
 | Exact fastener, grip, thread engagement, hole/locating-fit system, washers, preload method and retention
 | OEM mounting requirements plus combined-load, separation, slip and fatigue assessment
 | Chamber / reduction linkage
 | Certified force–stroke envelope, lever arms through travel, pin fits, bushings, stops and retained joints
 | Maximum and minimum service and spring forces; no overtravel or caliper overload with worn pads or faults
 | Wheelset and rotor hub
 | Wheel profile, journals, interference or other approved attachment, disc center plane, axial stack and runout
 | Wheelset specialist's combined bending/torsion/fatigue and rotor thermal design
 | Torque link and axlebox support
 | Articulated-end details, effective lever arm through movement, retention and clearances
 | Suspension geometry; reversible torque and impact envelope; bearing and local-frame calculations
 | Welded carrier subassembly
 | Qualified process, filler, joint preparation, weld sizes, sequence and inspection criteria
 | Approved material / fatigue detail and shop welding procedure; not a generic fillet note
Use the bearing journals to establish the axle axis, then control the disc center plane and carrier mounting face from that datum system. Record the complete axial stack rather than relying on a side-view envelope. Where finish-machined faces are part of a welded assembly, the engineer and shop must establish the weld/inspection/machining sequence and distortion allowance.
The manufacturing sequence is: verify actual OEM parts and drawings; resolve the chamber/caliper force and travel match; complete the wheelset and suspension interfaces; release the carrier and joint calculations; make a non-load-bearing fit fixture; fabricate and machine one prototype; inspect datums, fits and joints; cycle full suspension and pad-wear travel; perform instrumented static, reversible and fatigue/thermal testing to written criteria; release production after review. Farm-crew work can include the fit fixture, handling and controlled assembly after drawings are released. Wheelset manufacture, critical machining, qualified welding and final brake acceptance require the appropriate specialists.
Calculation audit
calculate_carrier_R0.py reproduces the JSON results. It checks load and moment equilibrium for every modeled bolt pattern and verifies the force/torque conversions. There are 213 passing arithmetic checks. These do not verify strength, fatigue life, stopping performance, suitability or approval. No plate thickness, bolt size or weld size has been inferred from the hole diameter.
NASA RP-1228 supplies the elastic bolt-group and combined-load framework; joint stiffness, preload, contact and fatigue remain explicit work items. AISC's HSS connection guidance identifies useful local support limit states, but it is not asserted to govern this vehicle or approve its mount. [MC01, MC04]

Custom wheels, axles and brake attachment — fabrication development
Revision PF-TRAIN-S3L-WHEEL-R0 • Reviewed 19 October 2026 UTC / 18 October 2026 Nevada time. Preliminary engineering for a fabrication package; production dimensions are not released.
What we will build
Develop a matched rigid wheelset: two custom-machined monobloc rail wheels on one engineered rotating axle, carried by bearings in the bogie. Start with a qualified railway wheel blank, with the flange formed as part of that wheel. The farm can build much of the surrounding vehicle; the wheel profile, wheel/axle fits, heat treatment and wheelset pressing belong with a rail wheel machine shop.
The brake connection is a designed part of this assembly. Develop either H-01A, a bolted disc on a machined integral wheel-hub mounting face, or H-01B, a separate engineered disc carrier on an axle seat. Do not weld a generic rotor carrier to an axle, drill an arbitrary bolt circle into a finished wheel, or assume a clamp collar can carry braking torque. The brake architecture team must choose one interface and close its torque, temperature, fatigue and service-access requirements before we finish the axle and wheel drawings.
This is a genuine custom manufacturing route: ORX's wheel page ↗ specifically describes machining wheel hubs for bolted brake discs and specialized profiles, and its wheelset page ↗ explicitly includes narrow gauge. Penn Machine ↗ offers custom forged solid wheels and design analysis. No supplier has yet accepted our dimensions, order size, application or delivery date. [WF01, WF03, WF04]
What carries forward
Phase 3G identified its wheel, axle and bearing shapes as symbols, not accepted hardware. Phase 3K used 0.25 and 0.30 m rolling radii only as calculations. Neither supplied a fabrication-ready wheel.
Retain the 36 in / 914.4 mm gauge evaluation, four-axle coach analysis and 32 in raised-floor study. Gauge alone determines neither wheel back-to-back nor axle length. The rail head, wheel contact profile, flange gauge point, turnout check gauge and flangeway all need a coordinated drawing. We must check new and worn geometry, tolerance extremes and lateral motion. Smaller wheels also change brake packaging, contact stress and rotational speed; a blank's availability does not establish suitability.
Loads already available to put on the engineering drawings
These are balanced, level-track analytical cases, not an accepted wheel/axle rating. Gravity is 9.80665 m/s².

For a regular coach with four equally braked axles, F_axle = m × a / 4 and T_axle = F_axle × D / 2:
 | Rolling-diameter probe
 | Torque/axle at 0.5 m/s²
 | Torque/axle at 0.7 m/s²
 | Speed at 7.5 mph
 | 500 mm
 | 412.5 N·m
 | 577.5 N·m
 | 128.1 rpm
 | 600 mm
 | 495.0 N·m
 | 693.0 N·m
 | 106.7 rpm
These demands exclude rotational inertia and unequal brake distribution. With equal left/right contact, each wheel contributes half the axle torque; a single axle disc and its carrier still transmit the whole axle torque. The joint and mount also require the maximum physically achievable brake torque, including parking application and credible simultaneous service/parking faults. Designing them only for the table would miss an important load case.
Before sizing the axle add the bearing-to-contact-point offsets, actual sprung/unsprung mass, lateral flange forces, track twist, load transfer, braking forces, impact/load factors and lifetime cycle spectrum. Existing coach-frame acceleration studies do not automatically define the correct wheelset fatigue loading.
The part drawings to finish
 | Part
 | Fabrication content
 | Evidence that sets the dimensions
 | W-01, wheel
 | Tread and flange coordinate profile; new/minimum worn diameters; rim width; bore; hub length/OD; web and fillet sections; disc mounting features if selected. Bore axis and back rim face are the principal drawing datums.
 | Matched rail/turnout geometry, wheel contact and fatigue analysis, braking thermal case, qualified blank and material class.
 | A-01, axle
 | Bearing journals, wheel seats, optional disc seat, shoulders, transitions, reliefs, end retention, common axis, lengths, surface finishes and runout controls.
 | Bearing selection and full combined-load/fatigue analysis, fit calculation and manufacturing process.
 | H-01, brake interface
 | Machined pilot/face and bolt group, or engineered press-fit carrier; axial location, runout, removal/tool clearance and thermal growth.
 | Selected rotor/caliper and rated torque, joint calculations and wheel/axle fatigue assessment.
 | WS-01, wheelset assembly
 | Back-to-back, wheel gauge at the defined profile datum, matched rolling diameters, bearing/disc positions, runout and serialized assembly acceptance.
 | One vehicle/track interface drawing, release tolerances and qualified assembly procedure.
The accompanying dimension matrix lists each held item explicitly. It does not disguise an unknown bore, fit or flange geometry as a shop dimension.
Material and process route
Preferred development route: forged/wrought monobloc wheel with integral flange, and a one-piece forged, heat-treated axle. ASTM A504/A504M-18(2023) ↗ is an active wheel specification covering material classes, treatment, hardness and inspection. ASTM A729/A729M-17(2022) ↗ covers heat-treated rail axles, including material/mechanical testing and nondestructive examination. These are candidate technical specifications, not asserted local legal requirements. We reviewed their public scope/abstract; the engineer and shop must obtain and use the full adopted specification. [WF06, WF07]
Do not select a wheel class solely because the train is slow, or specify ordinary 4140 bar as equivalent to a qualified railway axle. The required class, cleanliness, mechanical properties, treatment, fatigue performance and inspection must travel together. ORX's ability to machine several alloy families is manufacturing capability, not our material approval. [WF05]
Alternative: a qualified cast-steel rail wheel from a wheel manufacturer, with its own design and quality route. Amsted's published standard one-wear sizes start at 28 in, larger than the present 500/600 mm probes; its catalog is not evidence of a fitting small wheel. [WF10] A flame-cut plate wheel with a welded flange is not an established alternative in this package.
How the wheel shop makes and assembles it
Freeze interfaces: wheel/rail profile, diameter and worn limits, bearing arrangement, back-to-back, brake attachment and removal sequence.
Release drawings and process requirements: wheel/axle detail and assembly drawings, material/class, treatment, tolerances, NDT and acceptance records.
Buy traceable blanks: approved forging/casting source and stock allowance; heat/lot and material certificates.
Rough-machine and treat: follow the approved sequence; preserve final-machining stock; record treatment and test results.
Finish-machine: profile and fit surfaces, transitions, runout and finish; inspect and record each serialized part.
Match and press: measure each bore and seat, control their temperature, use approved tooling/lubricant and record force versus displacement. Reject/disposition an unacceptable fit; welding or set screws are not a remedy.
Install bearings and discs: follow the selected interface and OEM instructions; inspect retention, preload/locking, runout and free rotation.
First-article acceptance: dimensional record, instrumented brake torque and hot/cold interface checks, full movement clearance and engineer disposition before production.
This proposed traveler is supported by railway manufacturing practice. APTA's wheelset assembly standard ↗ addresses measured fits, pressing records and assembly qualification. Its large-axle tabulations must not be scaled into a guessed fit for this train. Its axle design practice, Rev. 2 ↗, provides a useful framework for seats, transitions, material/process control and fatigue. Responsible engineering must choose a coherent technical basis and confirm applicability. [WF08, WF09]
Calculations that turn the held fields into machining dimensions
For a preliminary solid circular axle section, nominal bending stress is 32M/(πd³) and nominal torsional stress is 16T/(πd³). These are starting equations only. Local transitions, press-fit fretting, surface condition, residual stress, corrosion and alternating loads determine fatigue suitability; a yield-strength check alone cannot release A-01.
For an ideal cylindrical friction fit, T = μ p π d² L / 2 and F_axial = μ p π d L. Actual interference requires shaft/hub elastic compatibility, material properties, geometry and hot/cold fit conditions. Unknown friction, hub stiffness and contact pressure are not substituted with convenient values. A wheel web and a short carrier can produce nonuniform pressure requiring a more complete model. Press force is a measured assembly acceptance variable, not an interchangeable torque rating.
Farm-crew scope now: build non-load-bearing fit mockups and protective handling aids to reviewed drawings; assist with later installation and inspection records under qualified supervision. Rail specialist scope: blanks, thermal processing, precision wheel/axle machining, NDT, pressing and wheelset dimensional acceptance. The custom brake bracket and bogie weldments can become local fabrication work after their calculations, drawings, weld procedures and inspection plan are released.
Next concrete decision: select the complete foundation brake and wheelset interface together, then freeze W-01/A-01/H-01 machining geometry. Do not order unmachined blanks until the wheel supplier confirms a viable blank and material/process route for that geometry.
